F Learning Symmetry Group Actions 

Roadmap. We begin by introducing notation and defining fibers, followed by a description of the learning curriculum and the key appearance events. We then break the training process into clearly defined phases and prove the main results for each phase: In Phase I, features emerge and compete; In Phase II, features grow and mutually cancel, with precise tracking of when different outcomes occur; In Phase III, features converge to a desired shape. Let us restate the action structure of G and Y here. 

Assumption F.1 (Assumption 5.1, restated) . Let LEGO (X , G, Y) be the LEGO language, where 

|Y| = nY 8 and G is a group acting on on Y. We assume the (left) group action α : G × Y → Y is transitive but not free. In particular, we assume nY = |Y| ∈ [ω(1) , O (log d)] and the group G satisfy 

nG = |G| = Θ(polylog( d)) > 1 

> ϱ

for all y ∈ Y .

Remark F.1. When the action α : G × Y → Y is transitive, there is only one orbit Y = {g · y | g ∈G} ≃ G /G y for all y ∈ Y . By orbit-stabilizer theorem, the stabilizer Gy at any point y has the same cardinality. 

Remark F.2. The canonical action of a symmetry group Sn on Zn satisfy Assumption F.1 with the choice ny = Θ( log log d  

> log log log d

) that keeps ny ! = O(polylog( d)) .

F.1 Preliminaries 

We work with fiber-indexed features. We first define fibers and super-combinations φ, then we define neuron features by its index ψ = ( j, r, φ ), and finally set the curriculum and appearance events in this notation. 

Definition F.1 (Fiber of values) . Assuming G follows Assumption F.1. For each j ∈ τ (Y) and y ∈ Y ,define the fiber of j at y as: 

Fiber j,y := {g ∈ G | τ (g · y) = j}

which collects all group elements that send y to y′ = τ −1(j).

Remark F.3. We denote Fiber j,y to be the set that transport y to y′ = τ −1(j) because it is the pre-image of the predictor map ϕy (·) : g 7 → τ (g · y) over y ∈ Y . Algebraically the fibers are the left-cosets {gG y }g∈G , where Gy := {g ∈ G | g · y = y} is the stablizer subgroup. Moreover, we have the following fact from the orbit-stablizer theorem. 

Fact F.1 (cardinality of the fibers) . For every j ∈ τ (Y) and y ∈ Y , |Fiber j,y | = |G| /|Y| = nG/n Y

from the orbit-stablizer theorem. 

Definition F.2 (Super-combinations) . Let (j, y ) ∈ τ (Y) × Y , we define the super-combinations ,which is a set of combinations:

F.1.1 Theorem Statement 

Now we present the theorem for learning symmetric group actions. 

Theorem F.1 (Learning Symmetric Group Actions) . Assuming Assumption 5.1, and let F (t) be obtained by Algorithm 2 at t = T1, then the loss for the 5th-token is minimized, i.e., Loss (T1)5 ≤ 1

The assertions of Theorem F.1 described the feature shapes of the neurons, which we formally defined in the proof of learning simply transitive actions (Definition E.1). The main difference between the results here and Theorem E.1 is that Theorem F.1C described a different symmetry of features without the factor Θ( ny ).Let us follow the proof in Appendix E and the feature shape for symmetry group actions. 

Definition F.6 (Feature shape) . Let ψ = ( j, r, φ ) ∈ Ψ, and let δ = ( δ1, δ 2) be the error parameters. We say the feature ψ has shape Fψ (δ) if the followings are satisfied: 1. Fψ, 1(δ1): V (t) 

> ψ

≥ B − O(δ1) and V (t) 

> ψ

≤ B + O(δ1);2. Fψ, 2(δ2): For any ψ′ = ( j, r, φ ′) ∈ Σ†,1 

> ψ

> j,r

(ϕ′)| ≤ O(δ2) for all ϕ′ ∈ φ′;3. Fψ, 3(δ2): |V (t) 

> j,r

(g) − CY V (t) 

> j,r

(y)| ≤ O(δ2) for any ϕ = ( g, y ) ∈ φ;4. Fψ, 4: For any feature ψ′ ∈ Σ†,3 

> ψ

, it holds that V (t) 

> ψ′

≤ eO(μ).Indeed, we aim to prove that Fψ (δ) holds for suitable parameter δ at the end of training, for every 

ψ ∈ Σ⋆. And this is indeed what the theorem states. 

Remark F.4. A major difference between Definition F.6 and Definition E.4 is that now the conditions involve not just one combination of weights, but all combinations ϕ ∈ φ given a combination φ ∈ Φ.This creates some significant difficulties for the proof.

Interpretations of Induction F.1. We explain what the inductions hypothesis mean here: (a) This property simply asserts that the good features never shrink below a certain level which is crucial for the optimization to work. (b) This property describes the curriculum nature of Σ⋆: each feature Vψ grows in different time intervals within 0 ≤ t ≤ T1.(c) This property asserts that the feature ψ′ previous to ψ in the curriculum Σ⋆ has stable and good feature shapes, which helps with learning the current feature. (d) This property means there are very few iteration where the gradient of Vj,r (ϕ), ϕ ∈ φ is disturbed before T 1 

> j,r,φ

is reached.

Proof of Inductions F.1 and F.2 in Phase I. Fix ψ = ( j, r, φ ) ∈ Σ⋆. We check items (a)-(d) in Induction F.1: • Induction F.1a: This is simple by combining lemmas F.1 and F.2, where each Vj,r (ϕ) is well approximated by a monotonically growing proxy eVj,r (ϕ).• Induction F.1b: This is proved later but here lemma F.3 showed that for any pair ψ ≺ ψ′ ∈ Σ⋆,that T 1

> ψ′

> ψ

> j,r

(y) + b5,j,r )q−1 because it grows slower than O(nY /n G) eVj,r (y) and thus Hoeffding’s inequality with variance parameter μ would work to bound the difference between the averaged gradient and the gradient with Vj,r (g) in expectation, and thus we obtain a bound

G Learning the Attention Layer: Simply Transitive Case 

In this section, we consider the case where the group operations form a simply transitive group. According to Assumption 4.1, we assume that for any y1, y 2 ∈ Y , there exists a unique g ∈ G 

such that g · y1 = y2. Without loss of generality, we let Y = {0, 1, . . . , n y − 1}, where ny ∈

[Ω(log log d), log d].We focus on updating only Q, while keeping W fixed. Combined with the attention structure specified in Assumption C.2, it suffices to consider the updates to the blocks Q4,3 and Q4,4 only. We consider the contribution to the gradient from the position i = 5 on task T 2; specifically, the relevant loss function is given by P2

G.2 Some Useful Bounds for Gradients 

In this subsection, we establish several useful bounds on the gradients of the attention layer, leveraging the feature structure of the MLP layer learned during stage 1. These bounds will be instrumental for the subsequent analysis. As established in Lemma E.7 and Theorem E.1, at the end of stage 1, the network exhibits the following activation properties: • Sparse activations: For each j ∈ τ (Y), and any feature (g, y ) ∈ Fj , there exists a unique 

activated neuron r ∈ [m] such that, under the event g1 = g and y0 = y, the following holds: 

Λ(T1)5,j,r ≥ B − O(δ), |V (T1) 

> j,r

(g) − V (T1) 

> j,r

(y)| ≤ O(δ)Λ(T1)5,j,r ′ ≤ O(δq−1) for all r′̸ = r. 

• Cancellation of incorrect features: let r ∈ [m] be the activated neuron associated with 

(g, y ) ∈ Fj . Then for any g′̸ = g ∈ G and any y′ ∈ Y , we have: 

V (T1) 

> j,r

(g) + V (T1) 

> j,r

(y′) ≤ O(δ),

V (T1) 

> j,r

(g′) + V (T1) 

> j,r

(y) ≤ O(δ),

In the analysis of FFN layer, we have a pair δ = ( δ1, δ 2); in the expressions above, some terms should use δ1 and others δ2. For brevity, we write δ := max {δ1, δ 2} and bound all such terms by δ. Since both δ1 and δ2 are small, this simplification does not affect our analysis. 

Notations for activated neurons. Since in the simply transitive case, the feature sets are disjoint across indices—i.e., Fj ∩ Fj′ = ∅ for all j̸ = j′—we denote the activated neuron corresponding to 70 (g, y ) ∈ Fj by rg·y . Moreover, let 

A ≜ ∪j∈τ (Y)Aj , where Aj ≜ {r | ∃ (g, y ) ∈ Fj , r = rg·y }.

In other words, A is the set of all activated neurons across all feature sets Fj for j ∈ τ (Y). Given 

ZL,ℓ −1, letting bG(ZL,ℓ −1) = ∪{ gℓ′ }Lℓ′=1 be the collection of all the chosen group elements in the predicate clauses. Similarly bY = ∪{ yℓ′ }ℓ−1

> ℓ′=0

. Then define bAj (ZL,ℓ −1) = {rg·y | (g, y ) ∈ Fj ∧  g ∈

bG(ZL,ℓ −1) ∨ y ∈ bY}. For simplicity, we omit the dependence on ZL,ℓ −1 in the notation of bAj when it is clear from the context. Equipped with these notations, we can summarize the above properties in the following lemmas. 

Lemma G.3 (Properties of target feature magnitude) . Given (g, y ) ∈ Fj with j ∈ τ (Y), then, the following properties hold. 

12

 Vj,r g·y (g) + Vj,r g·y (y) ≥ B − O(δ), Vj,r g·y (g) − Vj,r g·y (y) ≤ O(δ), (42) 

Vj,r g·y (g) + Vj,r g·y (y′) ≤ O(δ), V j,r g·y (y′) < 0 for all y′̸ = y, (43) 

Vj,r g·y (g′) + Vj,r g·y (y) ≤ O(δ), V j,r g·y (g′) < 0 for all g′̸ = g. (44) 

|Vj,r (g)| , |Vj,r (y)| ≤ O(δ) for all r / ∈ Aj . (45) 

Lemma G.4 (Properties of irrelevant magnitude) . If (p, v ) /∈ { 2} × G ∪ { 5} × Y , or j / ∈ τ (Y), then for any r ∈ [m], we have 

⟨W5,j,r,p , e v ⟩ ≤ eO(σ0). (46) The above lemmas give us some direct computations of the inner products between the weight matrices and input embedding vectors. 

Lemma G.5. Let j ∈ τ (Y) and ℓ ∈ [2] . Then for any r ∈ [m], the following holds: 

⟨W5,j,r , Zpred ,ℓ ⟩ = Vj,r (gℓ) ± eO(σ0), (47) 

⟨W5,j,r , Zans ,ℓ −1⟩ = Vj,r (yℓ−1) ± eO(σ0). (48) 

Moreover, for j / ∈ τ (Y) and any k ∈ I 2,1 and r ∈ [m], we have 

⟨W5,j,r , Zk⟩ = eO(σ0). (49) 

Proof. By direct computations and the definition of Zpred ,ℓ , we have 

⟨W5,j,r , Zpred ,ℓ ⟩ = ⟨W5,j,r, 1, e xℓ ⟩ + ⟨W5,j,r, 2, e gℓ ⟩ + ⟨W5,j,r, 3, e xℓ−1 ⟩

Plug in Lemma G.4 and the definition of Vj,r (gℓ), we obtain (47) . The proof of (48) and (49) is similar, and we omit the details here. Furthermore, we can establish some characterizations of the Λ5,j,r (Z2,ℓ −1) quantities, which are crucial for the following analysis.

G.3 Stage 2.1: Initial Growth of Gap 

At the beginning of stage 2, since Q has not been trained for long, we have the attention score is still close to the uniform structure. Therefore, for ℓ = 1 , we have 

Λ5,j,r (Z2,ℓ −1) ≈ 13 Vj,r (g1) + 13 Vj,r (g2) + 13 Vj,r (y0) ± eO(σ0).

Λ5,j,r (Z2,ℓ −1) ≈ 14 Vj,r (g1) + 14 Vj,r (g2) + 14 Vj,r (y0) + 14 Vj,r (y1) ± eO(σ0).

Here, activation means that the corresponding sReLU ′(Λ 5,j,r ) is non-zero, which is crucial for the gradient computation. Based on the above observations, we can see that the gradient from ℓ = 2 is relatively small since Λ is only activated in the smoothed regime. Thus, initially, the learning process is dominated by ∇QLoss 2,15 . Moreover, if we take a closer look at the gradient from ℓ = 1 , we have

This will lead to the growth of the gap between [Q4,3]s,s and [Q4,4]s,s .We formally characterize this growth behavior within this substage. At the beginning of each sub-stage, we establish an induction hypothesis that we expect to hold throughout. Subsequently, we analyze the dynamics under this hypothesis within the substage, aiming to prove its validity by the end of sub-stage. Due to the symmetry of [Q4,3]s,s and [Q4,4]s,s across s ∈ τ (X ), we may, without loss of generality, focus on a particular s ∈ τ (X ).

G.4 Stage 2.2: Continual Growth of Diagonal Entries 

In Stage 2.2, the diagonal entries [Q4,3]s,s and [Q4,4]s,s continue to grow until they reach a certain threshold. The analysis in this stage parallels that of Stage 2.1, but our focus now shifts to the gradients contributed by ℓ = 2 , as the logit at ℓ = 1 is already near-optimal and thus contributes negligibly to the growth of Q4,3 and Q4,4.

Induction G.2. Given s ∈ τ (X ), let T2,2,s denote the first time that [Q4,3]s,s reaches 0.0001 . For all iterations T2,1,s ≤ t < T 2,2,s , we have the following holds (a) [Q(t)4,3]s,s , [Q(t)4,4]s,s ≤ O(1) monotonically increases; (b) [Q(t)4,3]s,s − [Q(t)4,4]s,s ∈

Probabilistic events. We conclude this subsection by introducing several probabilistic events that will be used to simplify the characterization of activated neurons in the subsequent analysis. We first define some events that may contribute non-trivially to the gradient of Q4,3 and Q4,4:

E1 =

n

y0 = y1, g 1(y1)̸ = g2(y1)

o

, (105) 

E4 =

n

y0̸ = y1, g 1(yℓ−1) = g2(yℓ−1), for all ℓ ∈ [2] 

o

. (106) We first observe that event E1 occurs with high probability 1−O  1

> nc

, and serves as the primary regime of interest. In contrast, events E2 and E3 each occur with probability Θ(1 /n y ) and correspond to instances of initial prediction ambiguity, where certain incorrect classes may exhibit disproportionately large logits. Similarly, E4 corresponds to instances of initial prediction ambiguity, while occurs with probability Θ(1 /n 2 

> y

). Furthermore, we define the following events, which yield negligible gradient contributions to Q4,3 and Q4,4, as they do not lead to significant confusion among the incorrect predictions:

n

o

. (108) Here, E5 occurs with probability Θ(1 /n y ), and E6 occurs with probability Θ(1 /n 2 

> c

). Together, the events ∪i∈[6] Ei form a partition of the entire sample space. 

H.3 Stage 1.2.1: Initial Growth of Q

We define the following notations:

I Recursive Learning the Attention Layer: Symmetry Case 

In this section, we analyze the recursive learning dynamics of the attention layer in the symmetry case. To this end, we recall the definitions of the greedy data annotator, the bootstrapped LEGO data distribution, and the associated self-training loss. 

Definition I.1 (Greedy data annotator) . The greedy language model bpF induced by a network F is defined as 

bpF (Zans ,L ′+1 | ZL,L ′

) = 

1, if Zans ,L ′+1 = argmax Z pF (Z | ZL,L ′

),

0, otherwise. (118) 

Definition I.2 (Bootstrapped LEGO distribution) . We define DL,L ′ 

> F

as the LEGO distribution in Assumption 3.2, except that the answers Zans ,ℓ , 1 ≤ ℓ ≤ L′ are generated recursively by sampling 

Zans ,ℓ ∼ bpF (· | ZL,ℓ −1) from the greedy language model bpF .

Definition I.3 (Self-training loss) . Given a (fixed) model eF and length L, the self-training next-clause-prediction loss is defined by replacing DL,L ′

in Definition 3.7 with the bootstrapped distribution 

DL,L ′ 

> F

from Definition I.2: 

Loss L,L ′ 

> eF

(F ) ≜ EZL,L ′ ∼D L,L ′

> eF

h

− log pF (Zans ,L ′,i | ZL,L ′−1)

i

. (119) Similarly, the per-token loss is defined by 

Loss L,L ′ 

> eF ,i

= EZL,L ′ ∼D L,L ′

> eF

 − log pFi (Zans ,L ′,i | ZL,L ′−1), i ∈ [5] .

At stage k ≥ 2, let F (Tk−1) denote the model obtained from the previous stage, trained on the task 

T Lk−1 with Lk−1 = 2 k−1. Using the greedy annotator bpF (Tk−1) , we construct the bootstrapped LEGO distribution DLk ,L k  

> F(Tk−1)

with total length Lk = 2 k. The corresponding self-training loss 

Loss Lk ,L ′  

> F(Tk−1)

is then defined as in Definition I.3. In this stage, we focus on training via gradient descent on Loss Lk ,L ′  

> F(Tk−1),i

with sequence length L′ = 2 and target token i = 5 , initialized from the previous-stage model F (Tk−1).For notational simplicity, throughout the discussion we drop the subscript of the expectation operator 

E when Z is sampled from the ground-truth LEGO distribution; the subscript will be explicitly included only when the expectation is taken over the bootstrapped distribution. Moreover, we abbreviate the wrong attention error ϵLk ,2 

> attn

as ϵLk 

> attn

and the attention gap ∆Lk ,2 as ∆Lk when the context is clear. 138 I.1 Preliminaries 

We first present preliminaries on the recursive learning attention layer, including its gradient compu-tations and some useful probability lemmas. 

I.1.1 Gradient Computations Fact I.1 (Gradients of Q). Given F (Tk−1) from the previous stage T Lk−1 with Lk−1 = 2 k−1 for 

k ≥ 2, for (p, q ) ∈

n

(4 , 3) , (4 , 4) 

o

, we have 

−∇ Qp,q Loss Lk ,2  

> F(Tk−1),5

= −∇ Qp,q Loss Lk ,25 .

Remark I.1. This fact is straightforward to verify. Since the attention error ϵLk−1,2 

> attn

has already been controlled at a small constant level in the previous stage for the model F (Tk−1), when transitioning from length 2k−1 to 2k, the incorrect attention produced by F (Tk−1) cannot increase significantly. In particular, we have 

ϵLk 

> attn

≤ 2ϵLk−1 

> attn

,

which remains small. Moreover, the attention gap ∆Lk = |Attn ans ,1→pred ,2 − Attn ans ,1→ans ,1|

is non-increasing due to the doubling of irrelevant clauses. As a result, the probability assigned by F (Tk−1) to the correct prediction of Zans ,2,5 given ZLk ,1 remains significantly larger than that of any incorrect prediction. Consequently, under the greedy data annotator, the self-training loss 

Loss Lk ,2  

> F(Tk−1),5

coincides with the original loss Loss Lk ,25 on the ground-truth LEGO distribution.
